# Template-driven scaffolding of SCF(FBXO42) regulates PP2A degradation.
**Authors:** Coassolo S, Michaelian N, Maculins T, Azumaya CM, Cheung TK, Yin J, Zilberleyb I, Pahuja KB, Garner T, Lau T, Mau D, Grimmer M, Fortin JP, Costa M, Dimitrova YN, Rose CM, Hsu PL, Yauch RL

(#)Contributed equally

Protein phosphatase 2A (PP2A) is a Ser/Thr phosphatase that regulates the
phosphorylation of almost all cellular processes, including cell division and
proliferation1,2. PP2A forms heterotrimeric holoenzyme complexes comprising a
catalytic subunit (PP2Ac), a scaffolding subunit (PP2Aa) and variable B
regulatory subunits that exert precise control over enzyme substrate specificity
and prevent indiscriminate dephosphorylation of phosphoproteins3. However, the
mechanisms that control the activity of uncomplexed catalytic subunits have
remained relatively unclear. Here we find that the E3 ligase SKP1-CUL1-F-box
(SCF) complex containing F-box other protein 42 (FBXO42, also known as JFK;
hereafter, SCFFBXO42) degrades holoenzyme-free PP2Ac in a complex with the
coiled-coil protein CCDC6 to maintain cancer cell fitness. The cryo-electron
microscopy structure of the FBXO42-CCDC6-PP2Ac assembly reveals a
pseudosymmetric architecture in which CCDC6 forms a central dimeric template
that recruits multiple copies of PP2Ac and creates a substrate for FBXO42. Both
the quaternary structure of this CCDC6-PP2Ac heterodimer and the
post-translationally methylated tail of PP2Ac are recognized by FBXO42 for
ubiquitination. The multivalent structure facilitated by CCDC6 enables the
assembly of multiple degradation complexes along a single coiled coil, leading
to the turnover of free phosphatases and downregulation of catalytic activity.
Together, our findings define a mechanism for PP2A control through the
ubiquitin-proteosome system and establish a paradigm for cullin-RING
ligase-substrate interactions.

## Full Text

MainPP2A is a Ser/Thr phosphatase and a master regulator of the phosphoproteome that has an important role in various cellular processes, including cell cycle regulation, by dephosphorylation of key proteins4. Structurally, PP2A is a heterotrimeric complex comprising scaffolding (PP2Aa), catalytic (PP2Ac) and various B regulatory subunits. While the A and C subunits assemble to form the canonical core enzyme, the B subunits are exchanged to define phosphatase substrate specificity3. Given their key role in numerous cellular functions, PP2A complexes are tightly regulated through multiple mechanisms, including direct inhibition by other proteins and post-translational modification that signal for the assembly or disassembly of holocomplexes5–8. After disassembly, the activity of the PP2Ac catalytic subunit in isolation from the scaffold and B subunits must be kept in check to prevent unregulated and uncontrolled dephosphorylation of target proteins. While there have been links to proteasomal degradation as a means to tune PP2Ac levels5,9,10, the molecular mechanisms of PP2A regulation by the ubiquitin system are poorly understood.FBXO42 is a substrate-recognition component of the multi-subunit SCF complex, which has been previously reported to regulate the stability of both the wild-type (WT) and hotspot mutant forms of p5311–13 and several other putative substrates11,14–18. FBXO42 has recently been shown to be essential in a broad range of cancer cells and its depletion led to chromosome misalignments, cell cycle arrest and death in a glioblastoma model19. However, no known FBXO42 substrates have been connected to this phenotype to date, suggesting that uncharacterized substrates drive cancer cell survival.In an effort to understand FBXO42’s role in cancer cell fitness, we show that FBXO42 targets the catalytic subunit of PP2A for ubiquitination and degradation through a recognition mechanism that requires the assembly of a degradosome complex consisting of multiple PP2Ac monomers bound to the coiled-coil protein CCDC6. Notably, this assembly can be used to target the related PP4c, revealing a common mechanism that the cell uses to clear out unneeded phosphatases.

PP2A is a Ser/Thr phosphatase and a master regulator of the phosphoproteome that has an important role in various cellular processes, including cell cycle regulation, by dephosphorylation of key proteins4. Structurally, PP2A is a heterotrimeric complex comprising scaffolding (PP2Aa), catalytic (PP2Ac) and various B regulatory subunits. While the A and C subunits assemble to form the canonical core enzyme, the B subunits are exchanged to define phosphatase substrate specificity3. Given their key role in numerous cellular functions, PP2A complexes are tightly regulated through multiple mechanisms, including direct inhibition by other proteins and post-translational modification that signal for the assembly or disassembly of holocomplexes5–8. After disassembly, the activity of the PP2Ac catalytic subunit in isolation from the scaffold and B subunits must be kept in check to prevent unregulated and uncontrolled dephosphorylation of target proteins. While there have been links to proteasomal degradation as a means to tune PP2Ac levels5,9,10, the molecular mechanisms of PP2A regulation by the ubiquitin system are poorly understood.

FBXO42 is a substrate-recognition component of the multi-subunit SCF complex, which has been previously reported to regulate the stability of both the wild-type (WT) and hotspot mutant forms of p5311–13 and several other putative substrates11,14–18. FBXO42 has recently been shown to be essential in a broad range of cancer cells and its depletion led to chromosome misalignments, cell cycle arrest and death in a glioblastoma model19. However, no known FBXO42 substrates have been connected to this phenotype to date, suggesting that uncharacterized substrates drive cancer cell survival.

In an effort to understand FBXO42’s role in cancer cell fitness, we show that FBXO42 targets the catalytic subunit of PP2A for ubiquitination and degradation through a recognition mechanism that requires the assembly of a degradosome complex consisting of multiple PP2Ac monomers bound to the coiled-coil protein CCDC6. Notably, this assembly can be used to target the related PP4c, revealing a common mechanism that the cell uses to clear out unneeded phosphatases.

FBXO42 regulates cancer cell fitnessWhole-genome CRISPR loss-of-function screens previously identified FBXO42 as essential for a broad array of cancer cell lines; however, the underlying substrate necessary for maintaining cell fitness has not been identified. TP53 represents a putative candidate substrate given the previously defined role of FBXO42 in regulating both WT and hotspot point mutant p5311–13. However, we observed that the cellular dependency on FBXO42 can also occur in subsets of cancer cell lines with mutational loss of TP53 (Extended Data Fig. 1a). To validate this observation, we selected a sample of cell lines encoding WT or mutationally inactivated TP53 and evaluated the effect of small interfering RNA (siRNA)-mediated knockdown (KD) on clonogenic growth. FBXO42 KD was sufficient to substantially reduce clonogenic growth of cell lines with no functional TP53 and had no effect on growth of the selected TP53-WT cells (Extended Data Fig. 1b). FBXO42 KD resulted in mitotic arrest, as determined by immunofluorescence (IF) staining of a marker of mitotic chromosome segregation (phosphorylated histone 3 Ser10) (Extended Data Fig. 1c), accompanied by a specific blockade in prophase and enrichment of abnormal and polynucleated cells (Extended Data Fig. 1d–f). FBXO42 contains an N-terminal F-box motif that binds to the SKP1–CUL1–RBX1 scaffold, and a C-terminal Kelch β-propeller to recognize putative substrate proteins for ubiquitination11. To determine whether the loss of FBXO42 E3 ligase function is responsible for the growth defect, we derived stable clones overexpressing exogenous WT or truncated FBXO42 from TP53-mutated HOP-62 cells (Extended Data Fig. 1g). Cells were transfected with siControl or siFBXO42 targeting the endogenous gene, and confluency was monitored. As expected, the expression of WT FBXO42, but not an empty vector, rescued the FBXO42-KD phenotype (Extended Data Fig. 1h). Loss of either the SKP1-binding F-box motif or the putative substrate-binding Kelch domain did not rescue cell growth. This suggests that the phenotype is due to loss of FBXO42 E3 ligase function, probably owing to substrate stabilization. Together, these data demonstrate the essentiality of FBXO42 in the context in which TP53 is lost, suggesting that a critical and missing substrate protein(s) is regulated by FBXO42 in cancer cell growth.

FBXO42 regulates cancer cell fitness

Whole-genome CRISPR loss-of-function screens previously identified FBXO42 as essential for a broad array of cancer cell lines; however, the underlying substrate necessary for maintaining cell fitness has not been identified. TP53 represents a putative candidate substrate given the previously defined role of FBXO42 in regulating both WT and hotspot point mutant p5311–13. However, we observed that the cellular dependency on FBXO42 can also occur in subsets of cancer cell lines with mutational loss of TP53 (Extended Data Fig. 1a). To validate this observation, we selected a sample of cell lines encoding WT or mutationally inactivated TP53 and evaluated the effect of small interfering RNA (siRNA)-mediated knockdown (KD) on clonogenic growth. FBXO42 KD was sufficient to substantially reduce clonogenic growth of cell lines with no functional TP53 and had no effect on growth of the selected TP53-WT cells (Extended Data Fig. 1b). FBXO42 KD resulted in mitotic arrest, as determined by immunofluorescence (IF) staining of a marker of mitotic chromosome segregation (phosphorylated histone 3 Ser10) (Extended Data Fig. 1c), accompanied by a specific blockade in prophase and enrichment of abnormal and polynucleated cells (Extended Data Fig. 1d–f). FBXO42 contains an N-terminal F-box motif that binds to the SKP1–CUL1–RBX1 scaffold, and a C-terminal Kelch β-propeller to recognize putative substrate proteins for ubiquitination11. To determine whether the loss of FBXO42 E3 ligase function is responsible for the growth defect, we derived stable clones overexpressing exogenous WT or truncated FBXO42 from TP53-mutated HOP-62 cells (Extended Data Fig. 1g). Cells were transfected with siControl or siFBXO42 targeting the endogenous gene, and confluency was monitored. As expected, the expression of WT FBXO42, but not an empty vector, rescued the FBXO42-KD phenotype (Extended Data Fig. 1h). Loss of either the SKP1-binding F-box motif or the putative substrate-binding Kelch domain did not rescue cell growth. This suggests that the phenotype is due to loss of FBXO42 E3 ligase function, probably owing to substrate stabilization. Together, these data demonstrate the essentiality of FBXO42 in the context in which TP53 is lost, suggesting that a critical and missing substrate protein(s) is regulated by FBXO42 in cancer cell growth.

Identification of FBXO42 activity modifiersAs SCF complexes are generally acknowledged to initiate degradative ubiquitination20, we hypothesized that FBXO42 loss may result in stabilization of its substrate(s), thereby causing the resultant phenotype. To identify modifiers of FBXO42 function, we performed a genome-wide, pooled CRISPR knockout (KO) screen to identify gene KOs capable of overcoming the FBXO42-depletion phenotype.To this end, we generated a doxycycline (DOX)-inducible Cas9 clone of HOP-62 cells expressing a single guide RNA (sgRNA) targeting the FBXO42 gene (sgFBXO42), which resulted in protein loss and arrested cell growth (Fig. 1a,b). After introduction of a whole-genome sgRNA library, cells were treated with DOX for 10 days, and sgRNAs were sequenced to identify differential abundance (Fig. 1c and Methods). We detected significant enrichment for sgRNAs targeting the protein phosphatase 2 catalytic subunit alpha gene (PPP2CA), suggesting that depletion of PP2Ac protein could overcome the effect of FBXO42 KO (Fig. 1d and Supplementary Tables 1 and 2). Notably, our survival screen did not demonstrate any significant enrichment for sgRNAs targeting genes of the scaffolding or regulatory subunits of PP2A (Extended Data Fig. 2a). Nonetheless, sgRNAs targeting LCMT1, a positive regulator of PP2Ac activity6, were also found to be enriched, further suggesting that PP2Ac deregulation has a role in the observed phenotype (Fig. 1d and Extended Data Fig. 2b).Fig. 1FBXO42 targets the catalytic subunit of PP2A for ubiquitination to maintain cancer cell fitness.a, WB verification of FBXO42-KO efficiency in DOX-inducible CRISPR–Cas9 FBXO42-KO HOP-62 cells. b, Live-cell imaging analysis of cell confluency after DOX treatment in cells from a. Data are mean ± s.e.m. n = 3 biologically independent experiments. c, CRISPR KO survival screen workflow. The diagram was created using BioRender; Coassolo, S. https://BioRender.com/8flz312 (2026). d, CRISPR KO survival screen showing gene-level log2[fold change (FC)] versus −log[false-discovery rate (FDR)] of DOX-treated cells after 10 days versus the baseline. Puro, puromycin. e, Cell confluency was monitored using live-cell imaging after DOX-inducible FBXO42 KO in the presence of okadaic acid (OA). Data are mean ± s.e.m. n = 3 independent experiments. f, Volcano plot of the ubiquitinome detected by KGG-MS showing –log2[FC] (DOX versus DMSO) against significance (log10[P]) after DOX-inducible FBXO42 expression in HOP-62 cells (left). The red dots represent adjusted P < 0.05. n = 3 biologically independent samples. Right, the structure of PP2Ac with the ubiquitinated Lys34 highlighted by yellow spheres. g, WB analysis of the expression of PP2Ac and total ubiquitin from TUBE enrichment of DOX-inducible FBXO42 ORF HOP-62 nuclear lysates in the presence of MG132. h, WB of the V5 tag in nuclear lysates of HEK293T cells expressing exogenous V5-PP2Ac after siCtrl or siFBXO42 KD in the presence of MG132. i, WB analysis of the expression of Flag and PP2Ac in Flag–FBXO42–SKP1 recombinant protein IPs from HOP-62 nuclear lysates treated with DMSO, MG132 or MLN4924. j, WB of FBXO42 and PP2Ac from a panel of lung cancer cell lines after siCtrl or siFBXO42 KD for 72 h.Source dataTo test this hypothesis, we subjected DOX-inducible FBXO42 KO cells to treatment with okadaic acid—a broad-acting protein serine/threonine phosphatase (PPP) inhibitor. Owing to the cellular toxicity of this compound, we determined a concentration (10 nM) that had a minimal impact on HOP-62 cell proliferation but significantly reduced phosphatase activity in cells (Extended Data Fig. 2c,d). Okadaic acid treatment partially reversed the phenotype mediated by FBXO42 KO (Fig. 1e). Similarly, in a separate model (NCI-H1373 cells) in which a degradation tag (DTAG) was genetically knocked into the endogenous FBXO42 locus (DTAG–FBXO42), low dose okadaic acid treatment could further overcome the fitness effect caused by FBXO42 degradation mediated by the DTAGv1 ligand21 (Extended Data Fig. 2e,f). These results further indicate that the deregulation of protein phosphatases, in particular PP2Ac, is a key driver of the FBXO42-depletion phenotype.

Identification of FBXO42 activity modifiers

As SCF complexes are generally acknowledged to initiate degradative ubiquitination20, we hypothesized that FBXO42 loss may result in stabilization of its substrate(s), thereby causing the resultant phenotype. To identify modifiers of FBXO42 function, we performed a genome-wide, pooled CRISPR knockout (KO) screen to identify gene KOs capable of overcoming the FBXO42-depletion phenotype.

To this end, we generated a doxycycline (DOX)-inducible Cas9 clone of HOP-62 cells expressing a single guide RNA (sgRNA) targeting the FBXO42 gene (sgFBXO42), which resulted in protein loss and arrested cell growth (Fig. 1a,b). After introduction of a whole-genome sgRNA library, cells were treated with DOX for 10 days, and sgRNAs were sequenced to identify differential abundance (Fig. 1c and Methods). We detected significant enrichment for sgRNAs targeting the protein phosphatase 2 catalytic subunit alpha gene (PPP2CA), suggesting that depletion of PP2Ac protein could overcome the effect of FBXO42 KO (Fig. 1d and Supplementary Tables 1 and 2). Notably, our survival screen did not demonstrate any significant enrichment for sgRNAs targeting genes of the scaffolding or regulatory subunits of PP2A (Extended Data Fig. 2a). Nonetheless, sgRNAs targeting LCMT1, a positive regulator of PP2Ac activity6, were also found to be enriched, further suggesting that PP2Ac deregulation has a role in the observed phenotype (Fig. 1d and Extended Data Fig. 2b).Fig. 1FBXO42 targets the catalytic subunit of PP2A for ubiquitination to maintain cancer cell fitness.a, WB verification of FBXO42-KO efficiency in DOX-inducible CRISPR–Cas9 FBXO42-KO HOP-62 cells. b, Live-cell imaging analysis of cell confluency after DOX treatment in cells from a. Source data

To test this hypothesis, we subjected DOX-inducible FBXO42 KO cells to treatment with okadaic acid—a broad-acting protein serine/threonine phosphatase (PPP) inhibitor. Owing to the cellular toxicity of this compound, we determined a concentration (10 nM) that had a minimal impact on HOP-62 cell proliferation but significantly reduced phosphatase activity in cells (Extended Data Fig. 2c,d). Okadaic acid treatment partially reversed the phenotype mediated by FBXO42 KO (Fig. 1e). Similarly, in a separate model (NCI-H1373 cells) in which a degradation tag (DTAG) was genetically knocked into the endogenous FBXO42 locus (DTAG–FBXO42), low dose okadaic acid treatment could further overcome the fitness effect caused by FBXO42 degradation mediated by the DTAGv1 ligand21 (Extended Data Fig. 2e,f). These results further indicate that the deregulation of protein phosphatases, in particular PP2Ac, is a key driver of the FBXO42-depletion phenotype.

FBXO42 targets PP2Ac for degradationTo identify putative substrates of FBXO42 using an unbiased approach, we used KGG mass spectrometry (KGG-MS), which captures lysine residues carrying a diglycine (K-ε-GG) remnant after trypsin digestion, in engineered HOP-62 cells overexpressing DOX-inducible Flag–FBXO42 treated with MG132 to prevent rapid turnover of ubiquitinated proteins (Extended Data Fig. 3a,b). In summary, 26 proteins exhibited significant KGG peptide enrichment after FBXO42 overexpression. Importantly, the most significantly enriched ubiquitinated peptide aligned to PP2Ac (KQLSESQVKSLCEK34AK) (Fig. 1f and Supplementary Table 3). A few peptides that mapped to other proteins were also enriched (Extended Data Fig. 3c), but were not further investigated owing to their absence in our previous survival screen (Fig. 1d). To confirm this result, we used tandem ubiquitin-binding entities (TUBEs) to enrich for polyubiquitinated proteins in cell lysates after DOX induction of FBXO42. Consistent with the KGG-MS data, PP2Ac was enriched in TUBE pull-downs after FBXO42 overexpression (Fig. 1g), while siFBXO42 in cells expressing exogenous V5-PP2Ac demonstrated reduced ubiquitination (Fig. 1h), therefore confirming PP2Ac as a putative cellular substrate of FBXO42.To determine whether FBXO42 physically associates with PP2Ac, we incubated recombinant Flag–FBXO42–SKP1 protein in HOP-62 cell lysates, and performed a western blot (WB) analysis of PP2Ac after Flag immunoprecipitation (IP). We observed PP2Ac association only under conditions in which cells were pretreated with MG132 or a Neddylation inhibitor (MLN-4924) to hinder cullin-RING ligase activity (Fig. 1i), suggesting that PP2Ac is targeted by FBXO42 for proteasomal degradation. We next evaluated the PP2Ac half-life using a cycloheximide (CHX) chase assay to determine whether the PP2Ac half-life could be altered by FBXO42 depletion. Under normal conditions in HOP-62 cells, the PP2Ac half-life is approximately 36 h; FBXO42 depletion extends the PP2Ac half-life to nearly 60 h (Extended Data Fig. 3d). Furthermore, siRNA-mediated KD of FBXO42 in a small panel of lung cancer cell lines resulted in stabilization of the PP2Ac protein (Fig. 1j), suggesting that FBXO42-mediated cancer cell survival across these lines may involve a common mechanism.Notably, visualization of PP2Ac stabilization after depletion is most apparent in nuclear fractions, implying that FBXO42 may more strongly regulate the nuclear pool of PP2Ac (Extended Data Fig. 3e). For these reasons, certain experiments required nuclear fractionation for improved resolution, which is indicated in the corresponding figure legends.

FBXO42 targets PP2Ac for degradation

To identify putative substrates of FBXO42 using an unbiased approach, we used KGG mass spectrometry (KGG-MS), which captures lysine residues carrying a diglycine (K-ε-GG) remnant after trypsin digestion, in engineered HOP-62 cells overexpressing DOX-inducible Flag–FBXO42 treated with MG132 to prevent rapid turnover of ubiquitinated proteins (Extended Data Fig. 3a,b). In summary, 26 proteins exhibited significant KGG peptide enrichment after FBXO42 overexpression. Importantly, the most significantly enriched ubiquitinated peptide aligned to PP2Ac (KQLSESQVKSLCEK34AK) (Fig. 1f and Supplementary Table 3). A few peptides that mapped to other proteins were also enriched (Extended Data Fig. 3c), but were not further investigated owing to their absence in our previous survival screen (Fig. 1d). To confirm this result, we used tandem ubiquitin-binding entities (TUBEs) to enrich for polyubiquitinated proteins in cell lysates after DOX induction of FBXO42. Consistent with the KGG-MS data, PP2Ac was enriched in TUBE pull-downs after FBXO42 overexpression (Fig. 1g), while siFBXO42 in cells expressing exogenous V5-PP2Ac demonstrated reduced ubiquitination (Fig. 1h), therefore confirming PP2Ac as a putative cellular substrate of FBXO42.

To determine whether FBXO42 physically associates with PP2Ac, we incubated recombinant Flag–FBXO42–SKP1 protein in HOP-62 cell lysates, and performed a western blot (WB) analysis of PP2Ac after Flag immunoprecipitation (IP). We observed PP2Ac association only under conditions in which cells were pretreated with MG132 or a Neddylation inhibitor (MLN-4924) to hinder cullin-RING ligase activity (Fig. 1i), suggesting that PP2Ac is targeted by FBXO42 for proteasomal degradation. We next evaluated the PP2Ac half-life using a cycloheximide (CHX) chase assay to determine whether the PP2Ac half-life could be altered by FBXO42 depletion. Under normal conditions in HOP-62 cells, the PP2Ac half-life is approximately 36 h; FBXO42 depletion extends the PP2Ac half-life to nearly 60 h (Extended Data Fig. 3d). Furthermore, siRNA-mediated KD of FBXO42 in a small panel of lung cancer cell lines resulted in stabilization of the PP2Ac protein (Fig. 1j), suggesting that FBXO42-mediated cancer cell survival across these lines may involve a common mechanism.

Notably, visualization of PP2Ac stabilization after depletion is most apparent in nuclear fractions, implying that FBXO42 may more strongly regulate the nuclear pool of PP2Ac (Extended Data Fig. 3e). For these reasons, certain experiments required nuclear fractionation for improved resolution, which is indicated in the corresponding figure legends.

CCDC6 associates with FBXO42We were unable to biochemically isolate a stable complex between SCFFBXO42 and PP2Ac using recombinant proteins (Extended Data Fig. 4a); we therefore hypothesized that another protein may be necessary to facilitate stable complex formation between E3 and substrate. To explore this possibility, we immunoprecipitated a ligase-inactive Flag–FBXO42(ΔF-box) mutant in HOP-62 cells followed by global proteomic analysis using IP coupled with MS (IP–MS) to identify FBXO42 interactors. Relative to the control IP, we found strong enrichment for peptides corresponding to RBPJ, a previously identified FBXO42-interacting protein14, and CCDC6, a poorly studied coiled-coil protein (Fig. 2a and Supplementary Table 4). Notably, the effects of CCDC6 KO displayed an exceptionally strong correlation with FBXO42 KO in the Cancer Dependency Map (r = 0.75), suggesting a functional association between these proteins in addition to their physical interaction (Extended Data Fig. 4b). Using siCCDC6 in the same panel of FBXO42-sensitive cancer cell lines, we found that CCDC6 depletion phenocopies the FBXO42 KD viability effect, as well as mitotic defects (Extended Data Fig. 4c,d). While our data and the literature do not suggest that CCDC6 is a substrate of FBXO4219 (Fig. 1f), CCDC6 can co-IP with FBXO42–SKP1 only when cells are pretreated with either MG132 or MLN-4924, suggesting that this interaction is labile when ubiquitination and/or the proteasome is active (Fig. 2b). We found that PP2Ac could co-IP with Flag–CCDC6 in the absence of MG132 or MLN-4924 (Extended Data Fig. 4e), raising the possibility that FBXO42 is engaging PP2Ac bound to CCDC6. To investigate this, we immunoprecipitated FBXO42 from HEK293T cells ectopically expressing Flag–FBXO42 after siRNA-mediated KD of CCDC6. Notably, loss of CCDC6 protein completely abrogated the ability of FBXO42 to interact with PP2Ac (Fig. 2c). Similar results were obtained in HOP-62 cells expressing the ligase-dead FBXO42(ΔF-box) protein after CCDC6 siRNA KD (Fig. 2d). These results suggest that CCDC6 is required for the ability of FBXO42 to recognize PP2Ac in a broad range of cell types. To test this at the biochemical level, we set up an in vitro ubiquitination assay using purified components. To test this, we methylated PP2Ac (mPP2Ac) using recombinantly purified LCMT-1 (Extended Data Fig. 4f) and tested mPP2Ac binding to FBXO42 in the presence of CCDC6 using an in vitro homogenous time-resolved fluorescence (HTRF) assay. C-terminal carboxy-methylation of PP2Ac resulted in a considerable increase in HTRF signal, indicating that the methylation state of the PP2Ac C-terminal tail contributes to FBXO42 recognition (Fig. 2f).

CCDC6 associates with FBXO42

We were unable to biochemically isolate a stable complex between SCFFBXO42 and PP2Ac using recombinant proteins (Extended Data Fig. 4a); we therefore hypothesized that another protein may be necessary to facilitate stable complex formation between E3 and substrate. To explore this possibility, we immunoprecipitated a ligase-inactive Flag–FBXO42(ΔF-box) mutant in HOP-62 cells followed by global proteomic analysis using IP coupled with MS (IP–MS) to identify FBXO42 interactors. Relative to the control IP, we found strong enrichment for peptides corresponding to RBPJ, a previously identified FBXO42-interacting protein14, and CCDC6, a poorly studied coiled-coil protein (Fig. 2a and Supplementary Table 4). Notably, the effects of CCDC6 KO displayed an exceptionally strong correlation with FBXO42 KO in the Cancer Dependency Map (r = 0.75), suggesting a functional association between these proteins in addition to their physical interaction (Extended Data Fig. 4b). Using siCCDC6 in the same panel of FBXO42-sensitive cancer cell lines, we found that CCDC6 depletion phenocopies the FBXO42 KD viability effect, as well as mitotic defects (Extended Data Fig. 4c,d). While our data and the literature do not suggest that CCDC6 is a substrate of FBXO4219 (Fig. 1f), CCDC6 can co-IP with FBXO42–SKP1 only when cells are pretreated with either MG132 or MLN-4924, suggesting that this interaction is labile when ubiquitination and/or the proteasome is active (Fig. 2b). We found that PP2Ac could co-IP with Flag–CCDC6 in the absence of MG132 or MLN-4924 (Extended Data Fig. 4e), raising the possibility that FBXO42 is engaging PP2Ac bound to CCDC6. To investigate this, we immunoprecipitated FBXO42 from HEK293T cells ectopically expressing Flag–FBXO42 after siRNA-mediated KD of CCDC6. Notably, loss of CCDC6 protein completely abrogated the ability of FBXO42 to interact with PP2Ac (Fig. 2c). Similar results were obtained in HOP-62 cells expressing the ligase-dead FBXO42(ΔF-box) protein after CCDC6 siRNA KD (Fig. 2d). These results suggest that CCDC6 is required for the ability of FBXO42 to recognize PP2Ac in a broad range of cell types. To test this at the biochemical level, we set up an in vitro ubiquitination assay using purified components. Evidence in the literature has shown not only that PP2Ac is heavily C-terminally carboxy-methylated, but also that the methylation state of PP2Ac influences its proteolytic stability in cells6,22. To test this, we methylated PP2Ac (mPP2Ac) using recombinantly purified LCMT-1 (Extended Data Fig. 4f) and tested mPP2Ac binding to FBXO42 in the presence of CCDC6 using an in vitro homogenous time-resolved fluorescence (HTRF) assay. C-terminal carboxy-methylation of PP2Ac resulted in a considerable increase in HTRF signal, indicating that the methylation state of the PP2Ac C-terminal tail contributes to FBXO42 recognition (Fig. 2f).

Structure of the PP2Ac degradosomeTo understand the structural basis for CCDC6-dependent mPP2Ac ubiquitination by FBXO42, we biochemically reconstituted a complex between SCFFBXO42, CCDC6 and mPP2Ac for structural determination (Extended Data Fig. 4g). We solved the structure of an SCFFBXO42–CCDC6–mPP2Ac (FCP) complex to an overall resolution of 3.2 Å using cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM) (Fig. 3a and Extended Data Table 1). Focused refinements resulted in substantial map improvements, enabling de novo model building throughout the structure (Extended Data Figs. 5 and 6a–f). From the same dataset, we were also able to determine a structure of a CCDC6–PP2Ac (CP) subcomplex to an overall resolution of 3.5 Å (Extended Data Fig. 5).Fig. 3The overall structure of the FBXO42–CCDC6–PP2Ac degradosome.a, A composite cryo-EM map of the FBXO42–SKP1–CCDC6–mPP2Ac complex at an overall resolution of 3.2 Å. CCDC6 is shown in red, FBXO42 in slate, SKP1 in wheat and PP2Ac in grey. b, The structure of the FBXO42–SKP1–CCDC6–mPP2Ac complex shown from the same angle as in a (left) and rotated 90° to look down CCDC6 (right). The colouring of the subunits is identical to a. Unmodelled loops are represented as dashed lines. c, Schematic of the HTRF competition assay. The TR-FRET ratio was generated owing to the interaction between labelled Flag–HA–mPP2Ac (grey) and biotinylated FBXO42–SKP1 (slate and wheat) in the presence of untagged CCDC6 (red). Unlabelled FBXO42–SKP1 (faded slate and wheat) was titrated into the reaction inhibiting the TR-FRET ratio. The diagram was created using BioRender; Maculins, T. https://BioRender.com/zk6pu21 (2025). d, The nonlinear fit of the HTRF competition assay results performed at different mPP2Ac:CCDC6 ratios. The HTRF signal was normalized to the total FBXO42 (normalized HTRF signal = (observed HTRF signal/[FBXO42labelled]) × [FBXO42total]). n = 3 biologically independent experiments. Data are means ± s.d. Calculated KD values are reported in Supplementary Table 5. e, Cryo-EM map of 3×FBXO42–SKP1 bound to CCDC6–mPP2Ac at an overall resolution of 6.9 Å (left). Subunits are coloured as in a. Right, the structure of 3×FBXO42–SKP1-bound CCDC6–mPP2Ac rotated 90° relative to left panel with modelled CUL1–RBX1 (green and orange; Protein Data Bank (PDB): 1LDK). f, Superposition of a PP2a+c (PDB: 3FGA) heterodimer into the FCP complex. PP2Aa is shown in cyan as cartoon cylinders, and the FCP complex is shown as a transparent surface and coloured as in a. g, In vitro ubiquitination assay using recombinantly purified Flag–FBXO42–SKP1, CCDC6, PP2Ac and PP2Aa+c. Ubiquitination was detected using an antibody against PP2Ac.Source dataNotably, we found that CCDC6 forms a homodimeric parallel coiled-coil that runs down the centre of the complex with four pairs of PP2Ac bound to the sides of the coil (labelled motifs 1–4) (Fig. 3a,b). Two copies of FBXO42–SKP1 were observed to flank the PP2Ac motif 3 pair, with each copy of the E3 making contacts with both phosphatase protomers in the motif simultaneously. Comparison of the FCP complex structure with the CP subcomplex shows that binding of FBXO42 does not change the conformation of the CP complex, suggesting that CCDC6 assists in pre-arranging PP2Ac into a state that can be read by the E3 ligase (Extended Data Fig. 6g). To estimate the affinity of FBXO42 to CCDC6–mPP2Ac, we designed a competition HTRF experiment in which the assay signal is generated between fluorophore-labelled FBXO42 and mPP2Ac (Fig. 3c). In the absence of CCDC6, mPP2Ac shows low assay signal and weak binding to FBXO42. By contrast, the competition experiment in the presence of CCDC6 shows around 100 nM binding affinity between FBXO42 and mPP2Ac (Fig. 3d and Supplementary Table 5). Notably, altering the ratio between CCDC6 and mPP2Ac does not appear to change the affinity of FBXO42 to the phosphatase, suggesting no cooperative binding (Fig. 3d).Although PP2Ac is repeated multiple times throughout the structure, we observed no contacts between each pair of phosphatases per motif or across motif interactions (~15 Å spacing for motif 1–3) (Fig. 3b (left)). In addition to the spacing, each PP2Ac pair in motifs 1–3 is rotated approximately 20° to each other, while the distal motif 4 is rotated nearly 70° relative to its motif 3 neighbour (Fig. 3b (right)). From another dataset, we were able to determine a 6.9 Å structure of three copies of FBXO42–SKP1 bound to CCDC6–PP2Ac across motifs 2 and 3 (Fig. 3e (left) and Extended Data Fig. 7). Modelling in CUL1, we find that two CUL1 molecules can exist side by side, with minor clashes at the C terminus (Fig. 3e (right)). We suspect that the flexible CUL1 C-terminal domain can adjust positions to avoid steric clashes and facilitate ubiquitination.PP2Ac is generally thought to be bound constitutively to the PP2Aa scaffolding subunit before loading of a variable B subunit to define the PP2A holoenzyme3. To understand whether FBXO42 and CCDC6 could target PP2Ac in the context of a PP2Aa+c complex, we modelled a PP2Aa+c heterodimer into the structure. We observed severe clashes between PP2Aa, FBXO42 and a neighbouring copy of PP2Ac (Fig. 3f), suggesting that FBXO42 and CCDC6 target the catalytic subunit alone for ubiquitination. In an in vitro ubiquitination assay, we found that the PP2Aa+c complex is a poor substrate for FBXO42 compared with PP2Ac, probably owing to the competitive interactions between CCDC6–PP2Ac and PP2Aa+c (Fig. 3g). Overall, the structure points to a mechanism for recruitment of numerous PP2Ac monomers to a central CCDC6 scaffold for efficient ubiquitination and degradation by FBXO42, forming a large multi-SCF degradosome for PP2A catalytic subunits.

To understand the structural basis for CCDC6-dependent mPP2Ac ubiquitination by FBXO42, we biochemically reconstituted a complex between SCFFBXO42, CCDC6 and mPP2Ac for structural determination (Extended Data Fig. 4g). We solved the structure of an SCFFBXO42–CCDC6–mPP2Ac (FCP) complex to an overall resolution of 3.2 Å using cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM) (Fig. 3a and Extended Data Table 1). Focused refinements resulted in substantial map improvements, enabling de novo model building throughout the structure (Extended Data Figs. 5 and 6a–f). Notably, we found that CCDC6 forms a homodimeric parallel coiled-coil that runs down the centre of the complex with four pairs of PP2Ac bound to the sides of the coil (labelled motifs 1–4) (Fig. 3a,b). Two copies of FBXO42–SKP1 were observed to flank the PP2Ac motif 3 pair, with each copy of the E3 making contacts with both phosphatase protomers in the motif simultaneously. Comparison of the FCP complex structure with the CP subcomplex shows that binding of FBXO42 does not change the conformation of the CP complex, suggesting that CCDC6 assists in pre-arranging PP2Ac into a state that can be read by the E3 ligase (Extended Data Fig. 6g). To estimate the affinity of FBXO42 to CCDC6–mPP2Ac, we designed a competition HTRF experiment in which the assay signal is generated between fluorophore-labelled FBXO42 and mPP2Ac (Fig. 3c). In the absence of CCDC6, mPP2Ac shows low assay signal and weak binding to FBXO42. By contrast, the competition experiment in the presence of CCDC6 shows around 100 nM binding affinity between FBXO42 and mPP2Ac (Fig. 3d and Supplementary Table 5). Notably, altering the ratio between CCDC6 and mPP2Ac does not appear to change the affinity of FBXO42 to the phosphatase, suggesting no cooperative binding (Fig. 3d).

Although PP2Ac is repeated multiple times throughout the structure, we observed no contacts between each pair of phosphatases per motif or across motif interactions (~15 Å spacing for motif 1–3) (Fig. 3b (left)). In addition to the spacing, each PP2Ac pair in motifs 1–3 is rotated approximately 20° to each other, while the distal motif 4 is rotated nearly 70° relative to its motif 3 neighbour (Fig. 3b (right)). From another dataset, we were able to determine a 6.9 Å structure of three copies of FBXO42–SKP1 bound to CCDC6–PP2Ac across motifs 2 and 3 (Fig. 3e (left) and Extended Data Fig. 7). Modelling in CUL1, we find that two CUL1 molecules can exist side by side, with minor clashes at the C terminus (Fig. 3e (right)). We suspect that the flexible CUL1 C-terminal domain can adjust positions to avoid steric clashes and facilitate ubiquitination.

PP2Ac is generally thought to be bound constitutively to the PP2Aa scaffolding subunit before loading of a variable B subunit to define the PP2A holoenzyme3. To understand whether FBXO42 and CCDC6 could target PP2Ac in the context of a PP2Aa+c complex, we modelled a PP2Aa+c heterodimer into the structure. We observed severe clashes between PP2Aa, FBXO42 and a neighbouring copy of PP2Ac (Fig. 3f), suggesting that FBXO42 and CCDC6 target the catalytic subunit alone for ubiquitination. In an in vitro ubiquitination assay, we found that the PP2Aa+c complex is a poor substrate for FBXO42 compared with PP2Ac, probably owing to the competitive interactions between CCDC6–PP2Ac and PP2Aa+c (Fig. 3g). Overall, the structure points to a mechanism for recruitment of numerous PP2Ac monomers to a central CCDC6 scaffold for efficient ubiquitination and degradation by FBXO42, forming a large multi-SCF degradosome for PP2A catalytic subunits.

FBXO42 recognition of PP2AcFBXO42 contains an approximately 50-amino acid helical F-box domain that interacts with the SKP1 protein, while the substrate-recognition domain of FBXO42 forms a classical six-bladed Kelch β-propeller, where each blade comprises four anti-parallel β-strands (labelled A–D from the innermost strand)23 (Extended Data Fig. 8a,b).FBXO42 sandwiches a (CCDC6–mPP2Ac)2 heterotetramer from both sides (Fig. 4a), whereby one PP2Ac copy binds to the backside of the FBXO42 Kelch domain to function as an adapter, and a second copy inserts its tail into the hole of the β-propeller and is oriented towards RBX1 for ubiquitination (substrate) (Extended Data Fig. 8c). Owing to the symmetry of the assembly, the adapter–substrate pairs are reversed for the mirrored FBXO42 (Fig. 4a (faded copy)), allowing simultaneous ubiquitination of both phosphatases. An extended interface is formed between the backside of the Kelch domain with the PP2Ac adapter copy, centred on the 1BC and 1D–2A loops of FBXO42, and helix α10 of the phosphatase (Fig. 4b and Extended Data Fig. 8b). Moreover, FBXO42 inserts the 2BC loop into a crevice formed at the junction between CCDC6 and both PP2Ac monomers (Fig. 4c and Extended Data Fig. 8b), specifically interacting with the β8–β9 hairpin of the adapter PP2Ac and the α5 helix of the substrate copy. Notably, all of these loops are among the most well conserved elements of FBXO42 (Extended Data Fig. 8a).Fig. 4FBXO42 engages two PP2Ac monomers simultaneously.a, Overview of the (FBXO42–CCDC6–mPP2Ac)2 assembly, looking down CCDC6 near motif 3 (red cylinders). FBXO42 and SKP1 are coloured in slate and wheat, respectively, and PP2Ac is coloured in grey. The PP2Ac C-terminal YFLme peptide in the central hole of FBXO42 is shown as sticks. The symmetry copy of FBXO42–SKP1 is shown as a transparent cartoon. Unmodelled loops are represented by dashed lines. b, Magnified view of the FBXO42 backside (cartoon and surface) and PP2Ac adapter (grey) interface. Key residues are shown as sticks. c, Magnified view of the FBXO42 2BC loop and CCDC6–mPP2Ac interface. Key residues are shown as sticks. d, IP of Flag–FBXO42 loop deletions and WB of endogenous PP2Ac from HEK293T nuclear lysates in the presence of MLN-4924. e, In vitro ubiquitination assay using recombinant FBXO42 loop-deletion proteins. Ubiquitination was detected by blotting against the HA-tag of PP2Ac. f, Top view of the FBXO42 Kelch domain. Density for the PP2Ac YFLme peptide is shown as a red mesh, and the peptide is shown as grey sticks. g, Magnified view of the interface between PP2Ac YFLme tripeptide (grey sticks) and FBXO42 (slate). Density for the peptide is shown as a red mesh. h, IP analysis of V5-tagged WT or ΔC-ter PP2Ac with endogenous FBXO42 from HEK293T nuclear lysates in the presence of MLN-4924. Blots were probed with an antibody against FBXO42. i, In cell ubiquitination of ectopically expressed V5-PP2Ac WT and ΔC-ter with Flag–FBXO42 in nuclear lysates of HEK293T cells. WB was performed to detect V5-tagged PP2Ac and Flag-tagged FBXO42. j, In vitro ubiquitination assay using recombinantly purified His–PP2Ac with or without the C-terminal tail. Ubiquitination was detected by blotting against PP2Ac. k, The cell confluency at 7 days in HOP62 cells expressing Flag–FBXO42 WT or Kelch mutants W192A and G284F after endogenous FBXO42 KD (5′-UTR-targeting siRNA). n = 3 biologically independent experiments. Each bar represents the mean value. Statistical analysis was performed using one-way analysis of variance.Source dataTo understand whether the FBXO42 Kelch backside and 2BC loop interfaces are essential for binding and ubiquitination, we performed co-IP experiments with Flag-tagged loop deletions to pull-down endogenous PP2Ac. Deletion of the FBXO42 1BC loop on the backside ablated over 90% of the interaction, while loss of either the 2BC loop or a combination of deletions completely eliminated binding in cells (Fig. 4d). In an in vitro ubiquitination reaction, we observed that the FBXO42(Δ1BC) mutant can partially ubiquitinate PP2Ac, while the FBXO42(Δ2BC) and deletion combination fully abrogated activity (Fig. 4e). Together, these results indicate that the unique quaternary structure recognition mode of FBXO42 and the (CCDC6–PP2Ac)2 heterodimer is key for both in-cell target engagement and efficient enzymatic activity.

FBXO42 recognition of PP2Ac

FBXO42 contains an approximately 50-amino acid helical F-box domain that interacts with the SKP1 protein, while the substrate-recognition domain of FBXO42 forms a classical six-bladed Kelch β-propeller, where each blade comprises four anti-parallel β-strands (labelled A–D from the innermost strand)23 (Extended Data Fig. 8a,b).

FBXO42 sandwiches a (CCDC6–mPP2Ac)2 heterotetramer from both sides (Fig. 4a), whereby one PP2Ac copy binds to the backside of the FBXO42 Kelch domain to function as an adapter, and a second copy inserts its tail into the hole of the β-propeller and is oriented towards RBX1 for ubiquitination (substrate) (Extended Data Fig. 8c). Owing to the symmetry of the assembly, the adapter–substrate pairs are reversed for the mirrored FBXO42 (Fig. 4a (faded copy)), allowing simultaneous ubiquitination of both phosphatases. An extended interface is formed between the backside of the Kelch domain with the PP2Ac adapter copy, centred on the 1BC and 1D–2A loops of FBXO42, and helix α10 of the phosphatase (Fig. 4b and Extended Data Fig. 8b). Moreover, FBXO42 inserts the 2BC loop into a crevice formed at the junction between CCDC6 and both PP2Ac monomers (Fig. 4c and Extended Data Fig. 8b), specifically interacting with the β8–β9 hairpin of the adapter PP2Ac and the α5 helix of the substrate copy. Source data

To understand whether the FBXO42 Kelch backside and 2BC loop interfaces are essential for binding and ubiquitination, we performed co-IP experiments with Flag-tagged loop deletions to pull-down endogenous PP2Ac. Deletion of the FBXO42 1BC loop on the backside ablated over 90% of the interaction, while loss of either the 2BC loop or a combination of deletions completely eliminated binding in cells (Fig. 4d). In an in vitro ubiquitination reaction, we observed that the FBXO42(Δ1BC) mutant can partially ubiquitinate PP2Ac, while the FBXO42(Δ2BC) and deletion combination fully abrogated activity (Fig. 4e). Together, these results indicate that the unique quaternary structure recognition mode of FBXO42 and the (CCDC6–PP2Ac)2 heterodimer is key for both in-cell target engagement and efficient enzymatic activity.

FBXO42 reads the PP2Ac carboxy-methyl tailIn addition to the loops mediating PP2Ac recognition, we found density corresponding to the terminal YFLme tripeptide from the substrate copy of PP2Ac inserted into the central hole of the Kelch domain (Fig. 4f). Close examination reveals a claw-like conformation for the three PP2Ac residues (denoted by PP) in the pocket, with Tyr307PP and Phe308PP forming tight hydrophobic interactions with FBXO42 (denoted by F42) Trp192F42 and Leu678F42. The methylated Leu309PP (mLeu309) side chain is clamped between Pro305F42 and Trp340F42. Notably, the C-terminal carboxy-methyl is buried against a greasy glycine-rich cavity in the Kelch hole (Fig. 4g).Given our biochemical HTRF data demonstrating the boost in affinity between FBXO42 and PP2Ac after LCMT-1-mediated methylation (Fig. 2f), we examined whether the flexible C-terminal tail of PP2Ac was essential for recruitment to FBXO42. We generated V5-tagged PP2Ac with the C-terminal 14 amino acids removed, and transfected it into HEK293T cells to assess interaction with endogenous FBXO42. Despite only having a short motif inserted into the hole of the Kelch domain, the tailless PP2Ac was unable to IP FBXO42 from cell lysates (Fig. 4h). Cellular ubiquitination experiments also showed a substantial loss of PP2Ac ubiquitination with the tail deletion when overexpressed with Flag–FBXO42 (Fig. 4i). We also observed a total loss of ubiquitination when we used a tailless PP2Ac protein in an in vitro ubiquitination assay (Fig. 4j). Notably, a 14-amino acid carboxy-methylated peptide displayed weak (around 25 µM) affinity to FBXO42 in a surface plasmon resonance (SPR) experiment (Extended Data Fig. 8d and Supplementary Table 5). To test the importance of PP2Ac tail binding to the FBXO42 Kelch domain, we mutated the critical residues Trp192 and Gly284, which directly make contact with the PP2Ac tail, and overexpressed these mutants to assess their ability to compensate for the phenotype induced by siFBXO42. Consistent with our structural data, both mutants failed to rescue the phenotype caused by FBXO42 KD (Fig. 4k and Extended Data Fig. 8e). Together, our analysis indicates that FBXO42 uses its surface loops to recognize the quaternary assembly of a (CCDC6–PP2Ac)2 complex, and its central pocket to read and interpret the methylation state of the PP2Ac substrate.

FBXO42 reads the PP2Ac carboxy-methyl tail

In addition to the loops mediating PP2Ac recognition, we found density corresponding to the terminal YFLme tripeptide from the substrate copy of PP2Ac inserted into the central hole of the Kelch domain (Fig. 4f). Close examination reveals a claw-like conformation for the three PP2Ac residues (denoted by PP) in the pocket, with Tyr307PP and Phe308PP forming tight hydrophobic interactions with FBXO42 (denoted by F42) Trp192F42 and Leu678F42. The methylated Leu309PP (mLeu309) side chain is clamped between Pro305F42 and Trp340F42. Notably, the C-terminal carboxy-methyl is buried against a greasy glycine-rich cavity in the Kelch hole (Fig. 4g).

Given our biochemical HTRF data demonstrating the boost in affinity between FBXO42 and PP2Ac after LCMT-1-mediated methylation (Fig. 2f), we examined whether the flexible C-terminal tail of PP2Ac was essential for recruitment to FBXO42. We generated V5-tagged PP2Ac with the C-terminal 14 amino acids removed, and transfected it into HEK293T cells to assess interaction with endogenous FBXO42. Despite only having a short motif inserted into the hole of the Kelch domain, the tailless PP2Ac was unable to IP FBXO42 from cell lysates (Fig. 4h). Cellular ubiquitination experiments also showed a substantial loss of PP2Ac ubiquitination with the tail deletion when overexpressed with Flag–FBXO42 (Fig. 4i). We also observed a total loss of ubiquitination when we used a tailless PP2Ac protein in an in vitro ubiquitination assay (Fig. 4j). Notably, a 14-amino acid carboxy-methylated peptide displayed weak (around 25 µM) affinity to FBXO42 in a surface plasmon resonance (SPR) experiment (Extended Data Fig. 8d and Supplementary Table 5). To test the importance of PP2Ac tail binding to the FBXO42 Kelch domain, we mutated the critical residues Trp192 and Gly284, which directly make contact with the PP2Ac tail, and overexpressed these mutants to assess their ability to compensate for the phenotype induced by siFBXO42. Consistent with our structural data, both mutants failed to rescue the phenotype caused by FBXO42 KD (Fig. 4k and Extended Data Fig. 8e). Together, our analysis indicates that FBXO42 uses its surface loops to recognize the quaternary assembly of a (CCDC6–PP2Ac)2 complex, and its central pocket to read and interpret the methylation state of the PP2Ac substrate.

CCDC6 binds to PP2Ac through repeated motifsCCDC6 forms a coiled-coil that runs down the centre of the assembly and scaffolds multiple PP2Ac monomers along its length (Fig. 5a). Detailed examination of each bound copy shows a highly similar binding mode across all four motifs (Fig. 5b–e and Extended Data Fig. 9a (left)), where a set of CCDC6 acidic residues are engaging the active site of PP2Ac. Surface electrostatics of PP2Ac shows a highly basic cleft at the active site where two glutamates and an asparagine from CCDC6 are in direct contact with the deepest parts of the phosphatase (Fig. 5f). A sequence alignment of each motif in CCDC6 reveals a highly conserved stretch of residues defined by a EXXXEXXXN motif, whereby the strictly conserved Glu/Asn residues make specific interactions with PP2Ac, and flanking residues bolster the interface (Fig. 5f and Extended Data Fig. 9a (right) and 9b). This binding mode is highly reminiscent of several recently described structures of PP2A inhibitor proteins24,25, suggesting that CCDC6 also performs an inhibitory function, albeit through the degradation of the phosphatase (Extended Data Fig. 9c). Although we observed only four bound motifs in our structures, sequence analysis shows a fifth motif that is spaced C-terminally much further in the CCDC6 polypeptide (Extended Data Fig. 9a (right) and 9b) that is probably too flexibly tethered to the core of the complex for us to resolve using cryo-EM. In agreement with the structure, using HTRF, we found that CCDC6 robustly interacted with PP2Ac even in the absence of FBXO42 (Extended Data Fig. 9d).Fig. 5CCDC6 nucleates the complex assembly using conserved motifs.a, Overview of the interactions between a CCDC6 dimer (red) and multiple PP2Ac monomers (grey) shown as a cartoon. Key side chains in direct contact with PP2Ac are highlighted as green sticks. Ubiquitination was detected by using an antibody against PP2Ac. h, HTRF experiment showing the disassembly of the PP2A–B56 holoenzyme by CCDC6. The TR-FRET ratio was generated due to the interaction between labelled Flag–HA–mPP2Ac and His–PP2Aa, in the presence of untagged B56. Actin was used as the loading control for inputs.Source dataTo assess the importance of each motif in the ability of FBXO42 to recognize and ubiquitinate the CCDC6–PP2Ac multimer assembly, we made combination alanine mutants of each motif at positions 1, 4, 5 and 9 and tested their ability to stimulate PP2Ac ubiquitination. Relative to WT CCDC6, all five mutants disrupted ubiquitination of the phosphatase significantly (Fig. 5g). Although we did not observe FBXO42 bound at motif 4 or 5 in our structure (Fig. 3b (left) and Extended Data Fig. 5), our mutational data suggest that all five CCDC6 motifs are functional for supporting the ubiquitination of PP2Ac.Given that FBXO42 appeared to preferentially ubiquitinate PP2Ac outside the context of an assembled PP2Aa+c core enzyme (Fig. 3f,g), we examined whether CCDC6 could disrupt PP2A holoenzymes. First, we separately purified PP2Aa and B56, and established a binding assay for the complex by HTRF. When we titrated an increasing amount of CCDC6, we observed a loss of binding between PP2Ac and PP2Aa, indicating that CCDC6 can compete for catalytic subunits in the context of holoenzymes (Fig. 5h). Following up this observation, we wanted to investigate how much of PP2Ac is regulated by CCDC6 in cells. To estimate the fraction of PP2Ac bound by CCDC6, we immunodepleted CCDC6 using an HA-tagged CCDC6 homozygous knock-in model derived from NCI-H1373 cells (Fig. 5i). Immunodepletion was nearly complete and resulted in an approximately 15% reduction in total PP2Ac levels, indicating that around 15% of the cellular PP2Ac pool is bound to CCDC6 and available for regulation by SCFFBXO42 (Fig. 5i (right)). Our data collectively indicate that CCDC6 uses multiple PP2Ac-binding motifs to downregulate a select pool of phosphatases.

CCDC6 binds to PP2Ac through repeated motifs

CCDC6 forms a coiled-coil that runs down the centre of the assembly and scaffolds multiple PP2Ac monomers along its length (Fig. 5a). Detailed examination of each bound copy shows a highly similar binding mode across all four motifs (Fig. 5b–e and Extended Data Fig. 9a (left)), where a set of CCDC6 acidic residues are engaging the active site of PP2Ac. Surface electrostatics of PP2Ac shows a highly basic cleft at the active site where two glutamates and an asparagine from CCDC6 are in direct contact with the deepest parts of the phosphatase (Fig. 5f). A sequence alignment of each motif in CCDC6 reveals a highly conserved stretch of residues defined by a EXXXEXXXN motif, whereby the strictly conserved Glu/Asn residues make specific interactions with PP2Ac, and flanking residues bolster the interface (Fig. 5f and Extended Data Fig. 9a (right) and 9b). This binding mode is highly reminiscent of several recently described structures of PP2A inhibitor proteins24,25, suggesting that CCDC6 also performs an inhibitory function, albeit through the degradation of the phosphatase (Extended Data Fig. 9c). Although we observed only four bound motifs in our structures, sequence analysis shows a fifth motif that is spaced C-terminally much further in the CCDC6 polypeptide (Extended Data Fig. 9a (right) and 9b) that is probably too flexibly tethered to the core of the complex for us to resolve using cryo-EM.
